					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">President of the United States from 1857 to 1861</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the president of the United States. For other people with the same name, see <a href="/wiki/James_Buchanan_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="James Buchanan (disambiguation)">James Buchanan (disambiguation)</a>.</div>

<a href="/wiki/List_of_Pennsylvania_state_historical_markers" title="List of Pennsylvania state historical markers">Pennsylvania Historical Marker</a></div></th></tr><tr class="mergedrow"><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Official name</th><td class="infobox-data">James Buchanan</td></tr><tr class="mergedrow"><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Type</th><td class="infobox-data">Roadside</td></tr><tr class="mergedrow"><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Designated</th><td class="infobox-data">January 1955</td></tr><tr style="display:none"><th colspan="2">
</th></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">
</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>James Buchanan Jr.</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="&#39;b&#39; in &#39;buy&#39;">b</span><span title="/j/: &#39;y&#39; in &#39;yes&#39;">j</span><span title="/uː/: &#39;oo&#39; in &#39;goose&#39;">uː</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;k&#39; in &#39;kind&#39;">k</span><span title="/æ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;bad&#39;">æ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span></span>/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling">bew-<span style="font-size:90%">KAN</span>-ən</i></a>;<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> April 23, 1791&#160;&#8211;&#32;June 1, 1868) was an American lawyer, diplomat, and politician who served as the 15th <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">president of the United States</a> from 1857 to 1861. He previously served as <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State" title="United States Secretary of State">secretary of state</a> from 1845 to 1849 and represented <a href="/wiki/Pennsylvania" title="Pennsylvania">Pennsylvania</a> in both houses of the <a href="/wiki/U.S._Congress" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. Congress">U.S. Congress</a>. He was an advocate for <a href="/wiki/States%27_rights" title="States&#39; rights">states' rights</a>, particularly regarding <a href="/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States" title="Slavery in the United States">slavery</a>, and minimized the role of the <a href="/wiki/Federal_government_of_the_United_States" title="Federal government of the United States">federal government</a> preceding the <a href="/wiki/American_Civil_War" title="American Civil War">Civil War</a>. Buchanan was the last president born in the 18th century.
</p><p>Buchanan was a prominent lawyer in Pennsylvania and won his first election to the state's <a href="/wiki/Pennsylvania_House_of_Representatives" title="Pennsylvania House of Representatives">House of Representatives</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Federalist_Party" title="Federalist Party">Federalist</a>. He was elected to the <a href="/wiki/U.S._House_of_Representatives" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. House of Representatives">U.S. House of Representatives</a> in 1820 and retained that post for five terms, aligning with <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States)" title="Democratic Party (United States)">Democratic Party</a>. Buchanan served as Jackson's <a href="/wiki/List_of_ambassadors_of_the_United_States_to_Russia" title="List of ambassadors of the United States to Russia">minister to Russia</a> in 1832. He won the election in 1834 as a <a href="/wiki/U.S._senator_from_Pennsylvania" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. senator from Pennsylvania">U.S. senator from Pennsylvania</a> and continued in that position for 11 years. He was appointed to serve as President <a href="/wiki/James_K._Polk" title="James K. Polk">James K. Polk</a>'s secretary of state in 1845, and eight years later was named as President <a href="/wiki/Franklin_Pierce" title="Franklin Pierce">Franklin Pierce</a>'s <a href="/wiki/List_of_ambassadors_of_the_United_States_to_the_United_Kingdom" title="List of ambassadors of the United States to the United Kingdom">minister to the United Kingdom</a>.
</p><p>Beginning in 1844, Buchanan became a regular contender for the Democratic Party's presidential nomination. He was finally nominated in 1856, defeating incumbent Franklin Pierce and Senator <a href="/wiki/Stephen_A._Douglas" title="Stephen A. Douglas">Stephen A. Douglas</a> at the <a href="/wiki/1856_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1856 Democratic National Convention">Democratic National Convention</a>. He benefited from the fact that he had been out of the country as ambassador in London and had not been involved in slavery issues. Buchanan and running mate <a href="/wiki/John_C._Breckinridge" title="John C. Breckinridge">John C. Breckinridge</a> of Kentucky carried every slave state except Maryland, defeating anti-slavery Republican <a href="/wiki/John_C._Fr%C3%A9mont" title="John C. Frémont">John C. Frémont</a> and <a href="/wiki/Know-Nothing" class="mw-redirect" title="Know-Nothing">Know-Nothing</a> former president <a href="/wiki/Millard_Fillmore" title="Millard Fillmore">Millard Fillmore</a> to win the <a href="/wiki/1856_United_States_presidential_election" title="1856 United States presidential election">1856 presidential election</a>.
</p><p>As President, Buchanan intervened to assure the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States" title="Supreme Court of the United States">Supreme Court's</a> majority ruling in the pro-slavery decision in the <a href="/wiki/Dred_Scott_v._Sandford" title="Dred Scott v. Sandford"><i>Dred Scott</i></a> case. He acceded to Southern attempts to engineer <a href="/wiki/Kansas_Territory" title="Kansas Territory">Kansas'</a> entry into the Union as a <a href="/wiki/Slave_states_and_free_states" title="Slave states and free states">slave state</a> under the <a href="/wiki/Lecompton_Constitution" title="Lecompton Constitution">Lecompton Constitution</a>, and angered not only Republicans but also Northern Democrats. Buchanan honored his pledge to serve only one term and supported Breckinridge's unsuccessful candidacy in the <a href="/wiki/1860_United_States_presidential_election" title="1860 United States presidential election">1860 presidential election</a>. He failed to reconcile the fractured Democratic Party amid the grudge against <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Douglas" class="mw-redirect" title="Stephen Douglas">Stephen Douglas</a>, leading to the election of Republican and former Congressman <a href="/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln" title="Abraham Lincoln">Abraham Lincoln</a>.
</p><p>Buchanan's leadership during his <a href="/wiki/Lame_duck_(politics)" title="Lame duck (politics)">lame duck</a> period, before the American Civil War, has been widely criticized. He simultaneously angered the North by not stopping secession and the South by not yielding to their demands. He supported the ineffective <a href="/wiki/Corwin_Amendment" title="Corwin Amendment">Corwin Amendment</a> in an effort to reconcile the country. He made an unsuccessful attempt to reinforce <a href="/wiki/Fort_Sumter" title="Fort Sumter">Fort Sumter</a>, but otherwise refrained from preparing the military. His failure to forestall the Civil War has been described as incompetence, and he spent his last years defending his reputation. In his personal life, Buchanan never married and was the only U.S. president to remain a lifelong bachelor, leading some historians and authors to question his sexual orientation.  Buchanan died of respiratory failure in 1868 and was buried in <a href="/wiki/Lancaster,_Pennsylvania" title="Lancaster, Pennsylvania">Lancaster, Pennsylvania</a>, where he had lived for nearly 60 years. Historians and scholars <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">rank</a> Buchanan as one of the worst presidents in American history.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>James Buchanan Jr. was born April 23, 1791, in a <a href="/wiki/Log_cabin" title="Log cabin">log cabin</a> in <a href="/wiki/Cove_Gap,_Pennsylvania" title="Cove Gap, Pennsylvania">Cove Gap, Pennsylvania</a>, to James Buchanan Sr. (1761–1821) and Elizabeth Speer (1767–1833).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker20049–12_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker20049–12-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> His parents were both of <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Scots_people" title="Ulster Scots people">Ulster Scot</a> descent, and his father emigrated from <a href="/wiki/Ramelton" title="Ramelton">Ramelton</a>, Ireland in 1783. Shortly after Buchanan's birth, the family moved to a farm near <a href="/wiki/Mercersburg,_Pennsylvania" title="Mercersburg, Pennsylvania">Mercersburg, Pennsylvania</a>, and in 1794 the family moved into the town. His father became the wealthiest resident there, working as a merchant, farmer, and real estate investor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker20049–12_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker20049–12-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan attended the Old Stone Academy in Mercersburg and then <a href="/wiki/Dickinson_College" title="Dickinson College">Dickinson College</a> in <a href="/wiki/Carlisle,_Pennsylvania" title="Carlisle, Pennsylvania">Carlisle, Pennsylvania</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200412_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200412-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> He was nearly expelled for bad behavior but pleaded for a second chance and ultimately graduated with honors in 1809.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein19629–12_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein19629–12-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Later that year, he moved to the state capital at <a href="/wiki/Lancaster,_Pennsylvania" title="Lancaster, Pennsylvania">Lancaster</a>. James Hopkins, a leading lawyer there, accepted Buchanan as an <a href="/wiki/Reading_law" title="Reading law">apprentice</a>, and in 1812 he was admitted to the <a href="/wiki/Pennsylvania_Bar_Association" title="Pennsylvania Bar Association">Pennsylvania bar</a>. Many other lawyers moved to <a href="/wiki/Harrisburg,_Pennsylvania" title="Harrisburg, Pennsylvania">Harrisburg</a> when it became the state capital in 1812, but Buchanan made Lancaster his lifelong home. His income rapidly rose after he established his practice, and by 1821 he was earning over $11,000 per year (equivalent to $240,000&#32;in 2022). He handled various types of cases, including a much-publicized impeachment trial where he successfully defended Pennsylvania Judge <a href="/wiki/Walter_Franklin_(judge)" title="Walter Franklin (judge)">Walter Franklin</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200413–16_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200413–16-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan began his political career as a member of the <a href="/wiki/Federalist_Party_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Federalist Party (United States)">Federalist Party</a>, and was elected to the <a href="/wiki/Pennsylvania_House_of_Representatives" title="Pennsylvania House of Representatives">Pennsylvania House of Representatives</a> in 1814 and 1815.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECurtis188322_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis188322-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> The legislature met for only three months a year, but Buchanan's service helped him acquire more clients.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200418_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200418-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Politically, he supported federally-funded <a href="/wiki/Internal_improvements" title="Internal improvements">internal improvements</a>, a high <a href="/wiki/Tariffs_in_United_States_history" class="mw-redirect" title="Tariffs in United States history">tariff</a>, and a <a href="/wiki/History_of_central_banking_in_the_United_States" title="History of central banking in the United States">national bank</a>. He became a strong critic of <a href="/wiki/Democratic-Republican_Party" title="Democratic-Republican Party">Democratic-Republican</a> President <a href="/wiki/James_Madison" title="James Madison">James Madison</a> during the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200417–18_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200417–18-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was a <a href="/wiki/Freemason" class="mw-redirect" title="Freemason">Freemason</a>, and served as the Master of <a href="/wiki/Masonic_Lodge" class="mw-redirect" title="Masonic Lodge">Masonic Lodge</a> No. 43 in Lancaster and as a District Deputy <a href="/wiki/Master_Mason" class="mw-redirect" title="Master Mason">Grand Master</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Grand_Lodge_of_Pennsylvania" title="Grand Lodge of Pennsylvania">Grand Lodge of Pennsylvania</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein196227_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein196227-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Military_service">Military service</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Military service">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>When the British invaded neighboring <a href="/wiki/Maryland" title="Maryland">Maryland</a> in 1814, he served in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Baltimore" title="Battle of Baltimore">defense of Baltimore</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Private_(rank)" title="Private (rank)">private</a> in Henry Shippen's <a href="/wiki/Company_(military_unit)" title="Company (military unit)">Company</a>, 1st Brigade, 4th Division, Pennsylvania Militia, a unit of <a href="/wiki/J%C3%A4ger_(infantry)" title="Jäger (infantry)">yagers</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Buchanan is the only president with military experience who was not an officer.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> He is also the last president who served in the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Congressional_career">Congressional career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Congressional career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Electoral_history_of_James_Buchanan" title="Electoral history of James Buchanan">Electoral history of James Buchanan</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="U.S._House_service">U.S. House service</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: U.S. House service">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1820 Buchanan was elected to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="United States House of Representatives">U.S. House of Representatives</a>, though the Federalist Party was waning. As a young Representative, Buchanan was one of the most prominent leaders of the "Amalgamator party" faction of Pennsylvanian politics, named that because it was made up of both Democratic-Republicans and former Federalists. During the <a href="/wiki/1824_United_States_presidential_election" title="1824 United States presidential election">1824 presidential election</a>, Buchanan initially supported <a href="/wiki/Henry_Clay" title="Henry Clay">Henry Clay</a>, but switched to <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a> (with Clay as a second choice) when it became clear that the Pennsylvanian public overwhelmingly preferred Jackson.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> After the election, Buchanan continued supporting Jackson and helped organize his followers into the Democratic Party, afterwards becoming a prominent Pennsylvania Democrat. In Washington, Buchanan became an avid defender of <a href="/wiki/States%27_rights" title="States&#39; rights">states' rights</a>, and was close with many southern Congressmen, viewing some New England Congressmen as dangerous <a href="/wiki/Radical_politics" title="Radical politics">radicals</a>. He was appointed to the Agriculture Committee in his first year, and he eventually became Chairman of the <a href="/wiki/U.S._House_Committee_on_the_Judiciary" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. House Committee on the Judiciary">Judiciary Committee</a>. He declined re-nomination to a sixth term and briefly returned to private life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200423–30_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200423–30-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Minister_to_Russia">Minister to Russia</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Minister to Russia">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After Jackson was <a href="/wiki/1832_United_States_presidential_election" title="1832 United States presidential election">re-elected in 1832</a>, he offered Buchanan the position of <a href="/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_Russia" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Ambassador to Russia">United States Ambassador to Russia</a>. Buchanan was reluctant to leave the country but ultimately agreed. Buchanan had denounced Tsar <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_I_of_Russia" title="Nicholas I of Russia">Nicholas I</a> as a despot merely a year prior during his tenure in Congress; many Americans had reacted negatively to Russia's reaction to the <a href="/wiki/November_Uprising" title="November Uprising">1830 Polish uprising</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He served as an ambassador for 18 months, during which time he learned <a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Lingua_franca" title="Lingua franca">trade language</a> of diplomacy in the nineteenth century. He helped negotiate commercial and maritime treaties with the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200430–31_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200430–31-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:James_Buchanan_painted_by_J._Eichholtz.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/James_Buchanan_painted_by_J._Eichholtz.jpg/180px-James_Buchanan_painted_by_J._Eichholtz.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="230" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/James_Buchanan_painted_by_J._Eichholtz.jpg/270px-James_Buchanan_painted_by_J._Eichholtz.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/James_Buchanan_painted_by_J._Eichholtz.jpg/360px-James_Buchanan_painted_by_J._Eichholtz.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1095" data-file-height="1400" /></a><figcaption>1834 portrait of Buchanan at age 42–43 by <a href="/wiki/Jacob_Eichholtz" title="Jacob Eichholtz">Jacob Eichholtz</a></figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="U.S._Senate_service">U.S. Senate service</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: U.S. Senate service">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Buchanan returned home and was elected by the Pennsylvania state legislature to succeed <a href="/wiki/William_Wilkins_(U.S._politician)" class="mw-redirect" title="William Wilkins (U.S. politician)">William Wilkins</a> in the U.S. Senate. Wilkins, in turn, replaced Buchanan as the ambassador to Russia. The Jacksonian Buchanan, who was re-elected in 1836 and 1842, opposed the re-chartering of the <a href="/wiki/Second_Bank_of_the_United_States" title="Second Bank of the United States">Second Bank of the United States</a> and sought to expunge a congressional censure of Jackson stemming from the <a href="/wiki/Bank_War" title="Bank War">Bank War</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200430_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200430-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the contentious <a href="/wiki/1838_Pennsylvania_gubernatorial_election" title="1838 Pennsylvania gubernatorial election">1838 Pennsylvania gubernatorial election</a>, Buchanan chose to support the Democratic challenger, <a href="/wiki/David_R._Porter" title="David R. Porter">David Rittenhouse Porter</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> who was elected by fewer than 5,500 votes as Pennsylvania's first governor under the state's revised Constitution of 1838.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan also opposed a <a href="/wiki/Gag_rule_(United_States)" title="Gag rule (United States)">gag rule</a> sponsored by <a href="/wiki/John_C._Calhoun" title="John C. Calhoun">John C. Calhoun</a> that would have suppressed anti-slavery petitions. He joined the majority in blocking the rule, with most senators of the belief that it would have the reverse effect of strengthening the <a href="/wiki/Abolitionist" class="mw-redirect" title="Abolitionist">abolitionists</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> He said, "We have just as little right to interfere with slavery in the South, as we have to touch the right of petition."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200432_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200432-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Buchanan thought that the issue of <a href="/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States" title="Slavery in the United States">slavery</a> was the domain of the states, and he faulted abolitionists for exciting passions over the issue.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200430–38_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200430–38-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His support of <a href="/wiki/States%27_rights" title="States&#39; rights">states' rights</a> was matched by his support for <a href="/wiki/Manifest_Destiny" class="mw-redirect" title="Manifest Destiny">Manifest Destiny</a>, and he opposed the <a href="/wiki/Webster%E2%80%93Ashburton_Treaty" title="Webster–Ashburton Treaty">Webster–Ashburton Treaty</a> for its "surrender" of lands to the United Kingdom. Buchanan also argued for the annexation of both Texas and the <a href="/wiki/Oregon_Country" title="Oregon Country">Oregon Country</a>. In the lead-up to the <a href="/wiki/1844_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1844 Democratic National Convention">1844 Democratic National Convention</a>, Buchanan positioned himself as a potential alternative to former President <a href="/wiki/Martin_Van_Buren" title="Martin Van Buren">Martin Van Buren</a>, but the nomination went to <a href="/wiki/James_K._Polk" title="James K. Polk">James K. Polk</a>, who won the <a href="/wiki/1844_United_States_presidential_election" title="1844 United States presidential election">election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200430–38_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200430–38-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Diplomatic_career">Diplomatic career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Diplomatic career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Secretary_of_State">Secretary of State</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Secretary of State">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Polkcabinet1849edited.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Polkcabinet1849edited.png/222px-Polkcabinet1849edited.png" decoding="async" width="222" height="142" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Polkcabinet1849edited.png/333px-Polkcabinet1849edited.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Polkcabinet1849edited.png/444px-Polkcabinet1849edited.png 2x" data-file-width="1080" data-file-height="689" /></a><figcaption>Buchanan (second from the left) in Polk's cabinet, 1849</figcaption></figure><p>Buchanan was offered the position of Secretary of State in the Polk administration, as well as the alternative of serving on the Supreme Court. He accepted the State Department post and served for the duration of Polk's single term in office. He and Polk nearly doubled the territory of the United States through the <a href="/wiki/Oregon_Treaty" title="Oregon Treaty">Oregon Treaty</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Guadalupe_Hidalgo" title="Treaty of Guadalupe Hidalgo">Treaty of Guadalupe Hidalgo</a>, which included territory that is now Texas, California, Nevada, New Mexico, Arizona, Utah, and Colorado.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200438–40_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200438–40-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> In negotiations with Britain over Oregon, Buchanan at first preferred a compromise but later advocated for annexation of the entire territory. Eventually, he agreed to a division at the <a href="/wiki/49th_parallel_north" title="49th parallel north">49th parallel</a>. After the outbreak of the <a href="/wiki/Mexican%E2%80%93American_War" title="Mexican–American War">Mexican–American War</a>, he advised Polk against taking territory south of the <a href="/wiki/Rio_Grande_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Rio Grande River">Rio Grande River</a> and <a href="/wiki/Santa_Fe_de_Nuevo_M%C3%A9xico" title="Santa Fe de Nuevo México">New Mexico</a>. However, as the war came to an end, Buchanan argued for the annexation of further territory, and Polk began to suspect that he was angling to become president. Buchanan did quietly seek the nomination at the <a href="/wiki/1848_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1848 Democratic National Convention">1848 Democratic National Convention</a>, as Polk had promised to serve only one term, but Senator <a href="/wiki/Lewis_Cass" title="Lewis Cass">Lewis Cass</a> of Michigan was nominated.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200438–43_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200438–43-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ambassador_to_the_United_Kingdom">Ambassador to the United Kingdom</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Ambassador to the United Kingdom">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:James_Buchanan_sculpture_at_National_Portrait_Gallery_IMG_4538.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/James_Buchanan_sculpture_at_National_Portrait_Gallery_IMG_4538.JPG/220px-James_Buchanan_sculpture_at_National_Portrait_Gallery_IMG_4538.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/James_Buchanan_sculpture_at_National_Portrait_Gallery_IMG_4538.JPG/330px-James_Buchanan_sculpture_at_National_Portrait_Gallery_IMG_4538.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/James_Buchanan_sculpture_at_National_Portrait_Gallery_IMG_4538.JPG/440px-James_Buchanan_sculpture_at_National_Portrait_Gallery_IMG_4538.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4320" data-file-height="3240" /></a><figcaption>Bust of James Buchanan by <a href="/wiki/Henry_Dexter_(sculptor)" title="Henry Dexter (sculptor)">Henry Dexter</a> at the National Portrait Gallery</figcaption></figure>
<p>With the <a href="/wiki/1848_United_States_presidential_election" title="1848 United States presidential election">1848 election</a> of Whig <a href="/wiki/Zachary_Taylor" title="Zachary Taylor">Zachary Taylor</a>, Buchanan returned to private life. He bought the house of <a href="/wiki/Wheatland_(James_Buchanan_House)" title="Wheatland (James Buchanan House)">Wheatland</a> on the outskirts of Lancaster and entertained various visitors while monitoring political events.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200443–51_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200443–51-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> In 1852, he was named president of the Board of Trustees of <a href="/wiki/Franklin_and_Marshall_College" class="mw-redirect" title="Franklin and Marshall College">Franklin and Marshall College</a> in Lancaster, and he served in this capacity until 1866.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962210,_415_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962210,_415-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> He quietly campaigned for the 1852 Democratic presidential nomination, writing a public letter that deplored the <a href="/wiki/Wilmot_Proviso" title="Wilmot Proviso">Wilmot Proviso</a>, which proposed to ban slavery in new territories. He became known as a "<a href="/wiki/Doughface" title="Doughface">doughface</a>" due to his sympathy toward the South. At the <a href="/wiki/1852_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1852 Democratic National Convention">1852 Democratic National Convention</a>, he won the support of many southern delegates but failed to win the two-thirds support needed for the presidential nomination, which went to <a href="/wiki/Franklin_Pierce" title="Franklin Pierce">Franklin Pierce</a>. Buchanan declined to serve as the vice presidential nominee, and the convention instead nominated his close friend, <a href="/wiki/William_R._King" title="William R. King">William R. King</a>. Pierce won the <a href="/wiki/1852_United_States_presidential_election" title="1852 United States presidential election">1852 election</a>, and Buchanan accepted the position of <a href="/wiki/United_States_Minister_to_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Minister to the United Kingdom">United States Minister to the United Kingdom</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200451–58_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200451–58-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan sailed for England in the summer of 1853, and he remained abroad for the next three years. In 1850, the United States and Great Britain signed the <a href="/wiki/Clayton%E2%80%93Bulwer_Treaty" title="Clayton–Bulwer Treaty">Clayton–Bulwer Treaty</a>, which committed both countries to joint control of any future canal that would connect the Atlantic and Pacific Oceans through Central America. Buchanan met repeatedly with <a href="/wiki/George_Villiers,_4th_Earl_of_Clarendon" title="George Villiers, 4th Earl of Clarendon">Lord Clarendon</a>, the British foreign minister, in hopes of pressuring the British to withdraw from Central America. He also focused on the potential annexation of <a href="/wiki/Cuba" title="Cuba">Cuba</a>, which had long interested him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200458–65_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200458–65-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> At Pierce's prompting, Buchanan met in <a href="/wiki/Ostend" title="Ostend">Ostend</a>, Belgium, with U.S. Ambassador to Spain <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Soul%C3%A9" title="Pierre Soulé">Pierre Soulé</a> and U.S. Ambassador to France <a href="/wiki/John_Y._Mason" title="John Y. Mason">John Mason</a>. A memorandum draft resulted, called the <a href="/wiki/Ostend_Manifesto" title="Ostend Manifesto">Ostend Manifesto</a>, which proposed the purchase of Cuba from Spain, then in the midst of revolution and near bankruptcy. The document declared the island "as necessary to the North American republic as any of its present ... family of states". Against Buchanan's recommendation, the final draft of the manifesto suggested that "wresting it from Spain", if Spain refused to sell, would be justified "by every law, human and Divine".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcPherson1988110_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcPherson1988110-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> The manifesto, generally considered a blunder, was never acted upon. It weakened the Pierce administration and reduced support for Manifest Destiny.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcPherson1988110_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcPherson1988110-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETucker2009456–57_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETucker2009456–57-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidential_election_of_1856">Presidential election of 1856</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Presidential election of 1856">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1856_United_States_presidential_election" title="1856 United States presidential election">1856 United States presidential election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ElectoralCollege1856.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/ElectoralCollege1856.svg/330px-ElectoralCollege1856.svg.png" decoding="async" width="330" height="192" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/ElectoralCollege1856.svg/495px-ElectoralCollege1856.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/ElectoralCollege1856.svg/660px-ElectoralCollege1856.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1020" data-file-height="593" /></a><figcaption>1856 Map of electoral votes</figcaption></figure>
<p>Buchanan's service abroad allowed him to conveniently avoid the debate over the <a href="/wiki/Kansas%E2%80%93Nebraska_Act" title="Kansas–Nebraska Act">Kansas–Nebraska Act</a> then roiling the country in the slavery dispute.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200467–68_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200467–68-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> While he did not overtly seek the presidency, he assented to the movement on his behalf.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962248–252_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962248–252-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/1856_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1856 Democratic National Convention">1856 Democratic National Convention</a> met in June 1856, producing a platform that reflected his views, including support for the <a href="/wiki/Fugitive_Slave_Law" class="mw-redirect" title="Fugitive Slave Law">Fugitive Slave Law</a>, which required the return of escaped slaves. The platform also called for an end to anti-slavery agitation and U.S. "ascendancy in the Gulf of Mexico".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200469_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200469-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> President Pierce hoped for re-nomination, while Senator <a href="/wiki/Stephen_A._Douglas" title="Stephen A. Douglas">Stephen A. Douglas</a> also loomed as a strong candidate.
</p><p>Buchanan led on the first ballot, supported by powerful Senators <a href="/wiki/John_Slidell" title="John Slidell">John Slidell</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jesse_Bright" class="mw-redirect" title="Jesse Bright">Jesse Bright</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Thomas_F._Bayard" title="Thomas F. Bayard">Thomas F. Bayard</a>, who presented Buchanan as an experienced leader appealing to the North and South. He won the nomination after seventeen ballots. He was joined on the ticket by <a href="/wiki/John_C._Breckinridge" title="John C. Breckinridge">John C. Breckinridge</a> of Kentucky, placating supporters of Pierce and Douglas, also allies of Breckinridge.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200469–70_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200469–70-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan faced two candidates in the general election: former Whig President <a href="/wiki/Millard_Fillmore" title="Millard Fillmore">Millard Fillmore</a> ran as the American Party (or "<a href="/wiki/Know-Nothing" class="mw-redirect" title="Know-Nothing">Know-Nothing</a>") candidate, while <a href="/wiki/John_C._Fr%C3%A9mont" title="John C. Frémont">John C. Frémont</a> ran as the <a href="/wiki/U.S._Republican_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. Republican Party">Republican</a> nominee. Buchanan did not actively campaign, but he wrote letters and pledged to uphold the Democratic platform. In the election, he carried every slave state except for Maryland, as well as five slavery-free states, including his home state of Pennsylvania.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200469–70_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200469–70-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> He won 45 percent of the popular vote and decisively won the electoral vote, taking 174 of 296 votes. His election made him the first president from Pennsylvania.
</p><p>In a combative victory speech, Buchanan denounced Republicans, calling them a "dangerous" and "geographical" party that had unfairly attacked the South.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200470–73_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200470–73-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> He also declared, "the object of my administration will be to destroy sectional party, North or South, and to restore harmony to the Union under a national and conservative government."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962261–262_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962261–262-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> He set about this initially by feigning a sectional balance in his cabinet appointments.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200477–80_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200477–80-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Presidency_.281857.E2.80.931861.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidency_(1857–1861)">Presidency (1857–1861)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Presidency (1857–1861)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_James_Buchanan" title="Presidency of James Buchanan">Presidency of James Buchanan</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Inauguration">Inauguration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Inauguration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Inauguration_of_James_Buchanan" title="Inauguration of James Buchanan">Inauguration of James Buchanan</a></div>
<p>Buchanan was inaugurated on March 4, 1857, taking the oath of office from <a href="/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_the_United_States" title="Chief Justice of the United States">Chief Justice</a> <a href="/wiki/Roger_B._Taney" title="Roger B. Taney">Roger B. Taney</a>. In his inaugural address, Buchanan committed himself to serving only one term, as his predecessor had done. He expressed abhorrence for the growing divisions over slavery and its status in the territories while saying that Congress should play no role in determining the status of slavery in the states or territories.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200480–83,_85_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200480–83,_85-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> He also declared his support for <a href="/wiki/Popular_sovereignty_in_the_United_States" title="Popular sovereignty in the United States">popular sovereignty</a>. Buchanan recommended that a federal slave code be enacted to protect the rights of slaveowners in federal territories. He alluded to a then-pending Supreme Court case, <i><a href="/wiki/Dred_Scott_v._Sandford" title="Dred Scott v. Sandford">Dred Scott v. Sandford</a></i>, which he said would permanently settle the issue of slavery. Dred Scott was a slave who was temporarily taken from a slave state to a free territory by his owner, John Sanford (the court misspelled his name). After Scott returned to the slave state, he filed a petition for his freedom based on his time in the free territory. After Buchanan's speech, the Dred Scott case was decided against Scott and in favor of his owner.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200480–83,_85_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200480–83,_85-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
Associate Justice <a href="/wiki/Robert_C._Grier" class="mw-redirect" title="Robert C. Grier">Robert C. Grier</a> leaked the decision in the "Dred Scott" case early to Buchanan. In his inaugural address, Buchanan declared that the issue of slavery in the territories would be "speedily and finally settled" by the Supreme Court.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>  According to historian <a href="/wiki/Paul_Finkelman" title="Paul Finkelman">Paul Finkelman</a>:<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style></p><blockquote class="templatequote"><p> Buchanan already knew what the Court was going to decide. In a major breach of Court etiquette, Justice Grier, who, like Buchanan, was from Pennsylvania, had kept the President-elect fully informed about the progress of the case and the internal debates within the Court. When Buchanan urged the nation to support the decision, he already knew what Taney would say. Republican suspicions of impropriety turned out to be fully justified.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Historians agree that the Court decision was a major disaster because it dramatically inflamed tensions, leading to the Civil War.<sup id="cite_ref-Carrafiello-A_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Carrafiello-A-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> In 2022, historian <a href="/wiki/David_W._Blight" title="David W. Blight">David W. Blight</a> argues that the year 1857 was, "the great pivot on the road to disunion...largely because of the Dred Scott case, which stoked the fear, distrust and conspiratorial hatred already common in both the North and the South to new levels of intensity."<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Personnel">Personnel</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Personnel">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Cabinet_and_administration">Cabinet and administration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Cabinet and administration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
