Brown">Aaron V. Brown</a></th><td>1857&#8211;1859</td></tr><tr class="mw-empty-elt"></tr><tr><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Joseph_Holt" title="Joseph Holt">Joseph Holt</a></th><td>1859&#8211;1860</td></tr><tr class="mw-empty-elt"></tr><tr><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Horatio_King" title="Horatio King">Horatio King</a></th><td>1861</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Navy" title="United States Secretary of the Navy">Secretary of the Navy</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Isaac_Toucey" title="Isaac Toucey">Isaac Toucey</a></th><td>1857&#8211;1861</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Interior" title="United States Secretary of the Interior">Secretary of the Interior</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Jacob_Thompson" title="Jacob Thompson">Jacob Thompson</a></th><td>1857&#8211;1861</td></tr></tbody></table>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Buchanan_Cabinet.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/Buchanan_Cabinet.jpg/370px-Buchanan_Cabinet.jpg" decoding="async" width="370" height="273" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/ee/Buchanan_Cabinet.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="550" data-file-height="406" /></a><figcaption><i>President Buchanan and his Cabinet</i>, photograph by <a href="/wiki/Mathew_Brady" title="Mathew Brady">Mathew Brady</a> (c. 1859).<br />From left to right: <a href="/wiki/Jacob_Thompson" title="Jacob Thompson">Jacob Thompson</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lewis_Cass" title="Lewis Cass">Lewis Cass</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_B._Floyd" title="John B. Floyd">John B. Floyd</a>, James Buchanan, <a href="/wiki/Howell_Cobb" title="Howell Cobb">Howell Cobb</a>, <a href="/wiki/Isaac_Toucey" title="Isaac Toucey">Isaac Toucey</a>, <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Holt" title="Joseph Holt">Joseph Holt</a> and <a href="/wiki/Jeremiah_S._Black" title="Jeremiah S. Black">Jeremiah S. Black</a></figcaption></figure>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<p>As his inauguration approached, Buchanan sought to establish an obedient, harmonious cabinet to avoid the in-fighting that had plagued <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a>'s administration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200477_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200477-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> He chose four Southerners and three Northerners, the latter of whom were all considered to be <a href="/wiki/Doughface" title="Doughface">doughfaces</a> (Southern sympathizers).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200478_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200478-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> His objective was to dominate the cabinet, and he chose men who would agree with his views.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200479_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200479-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Concentrating on foreign policy, he appointed the aging <a href="/wiki/Lewis_Cass" title="Lewis Cass">Lewis Cass</a> as Secretary of State. Buchanan's appointment of Southerners and their allies alienated many in the North, and his failure to appoint any followers of <a href="/wiki/Stephen_A._Douglas" title="Stephen A. Douglas">Stephen A. Douglas</a> divided the party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200477–80_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200477–80-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Outside of the cabinet, he left in place many of Pierce's appointments but removed a disproportionate number of Northerners who had ties to Democratic opponents Pierce or Douglas. In that vein, he soon alienated their ally and his vice president, Breckinridge; the latter therefore played little role in the administration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200486–88_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200486–88-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Judicial_appointments">Judicial appointments</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Judicial appointments">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/List_of_federal_judges_appointed_by_James_Buchanan" title="List of federal judges appointed by James Buchanan">List of federal judges appointed by James Buchanan</a></div>
<p>Buchanan appointed one Justice, <a href="/wiki/Nathan_Clifford" title="Nathan Clifford">Nathan Clifford</a>, to the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States" title="Supreme Court of the United States">Supreme Court of the United States</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> He appointed seven other federal judges to <a href="/wiki/United_States_district_court" title="United States district court">United States district courts</a>. He also appointed two judges to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Claims" title="United States Court of Claims">United States Court of Claims</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Intervention_in_the_Dred_Scott_case">Intervention in the <i>Dred Scott</i> case</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Intervention in the Dred Scott case">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Two days after Buchanan was sworn in as president, Chief Justice Taney delivered the Dred Scott decision, which denied the petitioner's request to be set free from slavery. The ruling broadly asserted that Congress had no <a href="/wiki/U.S._Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. Constitution">constitutional</a> power to exclude slavery in the territories.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962316_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962316-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Prior to his inauguration, Buchanan had written to Justice <a href="/wiki/John_Catron" title="John Catron">John Catron</a> in January 1857, inquiring about the outcome of the case and suggesting that a broader decision, beyond the specifics of the case, would be more prudent.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962271–272_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962271–272-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Buchanan hoped that a broad decision protecting slavery in the territories could lay the issue to rest, allowing him to focus on other issues.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200483–84_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200483–84-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Catron, who was from Tennessee, replied on February 10, saying that the Supreme Court's Southern majority would decide against Scott, but would likely have to publish the decision on narrow grounds unless Buchanan could convince his fellow Pennsylvanian, Justice <a href="/wiki/Robert_Cooper_Grier" title="Robert Cooper Grier">Robert Cooper Grier</a>, to join the majority of the court.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHall2001566_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHall2001566-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Buchanan then wrote to Grier and prevailed upon him, providing the majority leverage to issue a broad-ranging decision sufficient to render the <a href="/wiki/Missouri_Compromise_of_1820" class="mw-redirect" title="Missouri Compromise of 1820">Missouri Compromise of 1820</a> unconstitutional.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPotter1976287_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPotter1976287-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200485_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200485-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Buchanan's letters were not then public; he was, however, seen at his inauguration in whispered conversation with the Chief Justice. When the decision was issued, Republicans began spreading the word that Taney had revealed to Buchanan the forthcoming result. Rather than destroying the Republican platform as Buchanan had hoped, the decision outraged Northerners who denounced it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200485–86_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200485–86-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Panic_of_1857">Panic of 1857</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Panic of 1857">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1857" title="Panic of 1857">Panic of 1857</a> began in the summer of that year, ushered in by the collapse of 1,400 state banks and 5,000 businesses. While the South escaped largely unscathed, numerous northern cities experienced drastic increases in unemployment. Buchanan agreed with the southerners who attributed the economic collapse to over-speculation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200490_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200490-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Reflecting his Jacksonian background, Buchanan's response was "reform not relief". While the government was "without the power to extend relief",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200490_61-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200490-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> it would continue to pay its debts in specie, and while it would not curtail public works, none would be added. In hopes of reducing paper money supplies and inflation, he urged the states to restrict the banks to a credit level of $3 to $1 of specie and discouraged the use of federal or state bonds as security for bank note issues. The economy recovered in several years, though many Americans suffered as a result of the panic.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962314–315_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962314–315-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Buchanan had hoped to reduce the deficit, but by the time he left office the federal deficit stood at $17&#160;million.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200490_61-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200490-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Utah_War">Utah War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Utah War">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Utah_War" title="Utah War">Utah War</a> and <a href="/wiki/Runaway_Officials_of_1851" title="Runaway Officials of 1851">Runaway Officials of 1851</a></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Utah" title="Utah">Utah</a> territory, settled in preceding decades by the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints" class="mw-redirect" title="Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints">Latter-day Saints</a> and their leader <a href="/wiki/Brigham_Young" title="Brigham Young">Brigham Young</a>, had grown increasingly hostile to federal intervention. Young harassed federal officers and discouraged outsiders from settling in the <a href="/wiki/Salt_Lake_City" title="Salt Lake City">Salt Lake City</a> area. In September 1857, the <a href="/wiki/Nauvoo_Legion#In_Utah" title="Nauvoo Legion">Utah Territorial Militia</a>, associated with the Latter-day Saints, perpetrated the <a href="/wiki/Mountain_Meadows_massacre" class="mw-redirect" title="Mountain Meadows massacre">Mountain Meadows massacre</a> against Arkansans headed for California. Buchanan was offended by the militarism and <a href="/wiki/Mormonism_and_polygamy" title="Mormonism and polygamy">polygamous</a> behavior of Young.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200490–91_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200490–91-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Believing the Latter-day Saints to be in open rebellion, Buchanan in July 1857 sent <a href="/wiki/Alfred_Cumming_(governor)" title="Alfred Cumming (governor)">Alfred Cumming</a>, accompanied by the Army, to replace Young as governor. While the Latter-day Saints had frequently defied federal authority, some historians consider Buchanan's action was an inappropriate response to uncorroborated reports.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962316_54-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962316-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Complicating matters, Young's notice of his replacement was not delivered because the Pierce administration had annulled the Utah mail contract.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962316_54-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962316-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Young reacted to the military action by mustering a two-week expedition, destroying wagon trains, oxen, and other Army property. Buchanan then dispatched <a href="/wiki/Thomas_L._Kane" title="Thomas L. Kane">Thomas L. Kane</a> as a private agent to negotiate peace. The mission succeeded, the new governor took office, and the <a href="/wiki/Utah_War" title="Utah War">Utah War</a> ended. The President granted amnesty to inhabitants affirming loyalty to the government, and placed the federal troops at a peaceable distance for the balance of his administration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962317_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962317-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Transatlantic_telegraph_cable"><a href="/wiki/Transatlantic_telegraph_cable" title="Transatlantic telegraph cable">Transatlantic telegraph cable</a></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Transatlantic telegraph cable">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Buchanan was the first recipient of an official <a href="/wiki/Telegram" class="mw-redirect" title="Telegram">telegram</a> transmitted across the Atlantic.  Following the dispatch of test and configuration telegrams, on 16 August 1858 <a href="/wiki/Queen_Victoria" title="Queen Victoria">Queen Victoria</a> sent a 98 word message to Buchanan at his summer residence in the <a href="/wiki/Bedford_Springs_Hotel" class="mw-redirect" title="Bedford Springs Hotel">Bedford Springs Hotel</a> in <a href="/wiki/Pennsylvania" title="Pennsylvania">Pennsylvania</a>, expressing hope that the newly laid cable would prove "an additional link between the nations whose friendship is founded on their common interest and reciprocal esteem".   Queen Victoria's message took 16&#160;hours to send.<sup id="cite_ref-Trade1861_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Trade1861-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-bbc-saw-2_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bbc-saw-2-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>  
</p><p>Buchanan responded: "It is a triumph more glorious, because far more useful to mankind, than was ever won by conqueror on the field of battle.  May the Atlantic telegraph, under the blessing of Heaven, prove to be a bond of perpetual peace and friendship between the kindred nations, and an instrument destined by Divine Providence to diffuse religion, civilization, liberty, and law throughout the world."<sup id="cite_ref-spencer-vol3_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-spencer-vol3-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><br />
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Bleeding_Kansas">Bleeding Kansas</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Bleeding Kansas">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Bleeding_Kansas" title="Bleeding Kansas">Bleeding Kansas</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:US_SlaveFree1858.gif" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/US_SlaveFree1858.gif/350px-US_SlaveFree1858.gif" decoding="async" width="350" height="212" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/US_SlaveFree1858.gif/525px-US_SlaveFree1858.gif 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/US_SlaveFree1858.gif/700px-US_SlaveFree1858.gif 2x" data-file-width="774" data-file-height="468" /></a><figcaption>The balance of free and slave states and territories in 1858, after the admission of <a href="/wiki/Minnesota" title="Minnesota">Minnesota</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Kansas%E2%80%93Nebraska_Act" title="Kansas–Nebraska Act">Kansas–Nebraska Act</a> of 1854 created the <a href="/wiki/Kansas_Territory" title="Kansas Territory">Kansas Territory</a> and allowed the settlers there to decide whether to allow slavery. This resulted in violence between "<a href="/wiki/Free-Soil" class="mw-redirect" title="Free-Soil">Free-Soil</a>" (antislavery) and pro-slavery settlers, which developed into the "<a href="/wiki/Bleeding_Kansas" title="Bleeding Kansas">Bleeding Kansas</a>" period. The antislavery settlers, with the help of Northern abolitionists, organized a government in <a href="/wiki/Topeka" class="mw-redirect" title="Topeka">Topeka</a>. The more numerous proslavery settlers, many from the neighboring slave state Missouri, established a government in <a href="/wiki/Lecompton,_Kansas" title="Lecompton, Kansas">Lecompton</a>, giving the Territory two different governments for a time, with two distinct constitutions, each claiming legitimacy.
</p><p>The admission of Kansas as a state required a constitution be submitted to Congress with the approval of a majority of its residents. Under President Pierce, a series of violent confrontations escalated over who had the right to vote in Kansas. The situation drew national attention, and some in Georgia and Mississippi advocated <a href="/wiki/Secession_in_the_United_States" title="Secession in the United States">secession</a> should Kansas be admitted as a free state. Buchanan chose to endorse the pro-slavery Lecompton government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200493–98_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200493–98-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan appointed <a href="/wiki/Robert_J._Walker" title="Robert J. Walker">Robert J. Walker</a> to replace <a href="/wiki/John_W._Geary" title="John W. Geary">John W. Geary</a> as Territorial Governor,  and there ensued conflicting referendums from Topeka and Lecompton, where election fraud occurred. In October 1857, the Lecompton government framed the pro-slavery <a href="/wiki/Lecompton_Constitution" title="Lecompton Constitution">Lecompton Constitution</a> agreed to a referendum limited solely to the slavery question. However, the vote against slavery, as provided by the Lecompton Convention, would still permit existing slaves, and all their issue, to be enslaved, so there was no referendum that permitted the majority anti-slavery residents to prohibit slavery in Kansas. As a result anti-slavery residents boycotted the referendum since it did not provide a meaningful choice.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200497–100_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200497–100-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite the protests of Walker and two former Kansas governors, Buchanan decided to accept the Lecompton Constitution. In a December 1857 meeting with Stephen Douglas, the chairman of the Senate Committee on Territories, Buchanan demanded that all Democrats support the administration's position of admitting Kansas under the Lecompton Constitution. On February 2, he transmitted the Lecompton Constitution to Congress. He also transmitted a message that attacked the "revolutionary government" in Topeka, conflating them with the Mormons in Utah. Buchanan made every effort to secure congressional approval, offering favors, patronage appointments, and even cash for votes. The Lecompton Constitution won the approval of the Senate in March, but a combination of Know-Nothings, Republicans, and northern Democrats defeated the bill in the House. Rather than accepting defeat, Buchanan backed the 1858 <a href="/wiki/English_Bill_(1858)" title="English Bill (1858)">English Bill</a>, which offered Kansans immediate statehood and vast public lands in exchange for accepting the Lecompton Constitution. In August 1858, Kansans by referendum strongly rejected the Lecompton Constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker2004100–105_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker2004100–105-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The dispute over Kansas became the battlefront for control of the Democratic Party. On one side were Buchanan, most Southern Democrats, and the "doughfaces". On the other side were Douglas and most northern Democrats plus a few Southerners. Douglas's faction continued to support the doctrine of popular sovereignty, while Buchanan insisted that Democrats respect the <i>Dred Scott</i> decision and its repudiation of federal interference with slavery in the territories.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker2004120–121_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker2004120–121-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> The struggle ended only with Buchanan's presidency. In the interim he used his patronage powers to remove Douglas sympathizers in Illinois and Washington, D.C., and installed pro-administration Democrats, including postmasters.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChadwick200891_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChadwick200891-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1858_mid-term_elections">1858 mid-term elections</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: 1858 mid-term elections">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Douglas's Senate term was coming to an end in 1859, with the Illinois legislature, elected in 1858, determining whether Douglas would win re-election. The Senate seat was the primary issue of the legislative election, marked by the famous <a href="/wiki/Lincoln-Douglas_debates" class="mw-redirect" title="Lincoln-Douglas debates">debates</a> between Douglas and his Republican opponent for the seat, <a href="/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln" title="Abraham Lincoln">Abraham Lincoln</a>. Buchanan, working through federal patronage appointees in Illinois, ran candidates for the legislature in competition with both the Republicans and the Douglas Democrats. This could easily have thrown the election to the Republicans, and showed the depth of Buchanan's animosity toward Douglas.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChadwick2008117_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChadwick2008117-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> In the end, Douglas Democrats won the legislative election and Douglas was re-elected to the Senate. In that year's elections, Douglas forces took control throughout the North, except in Buchanan's home state of Pennsylvania. Buchanan's support was otherwise reduced to a narrow base of southerners.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPotter1976297–327_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPotter1976297–327-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962286–299_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962286–299-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The division between northern and southern Democrats allowed the Republicans to win a plurality of the House in the <a href="/wiki/1858_United_States_elections" title="1858 United States elections">1858 elections</a>, and allowed them to block most of Buchanan's agenda. Buchanan, in turn, added to the hostility with his veto of six substantial pieces of Republican legislation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962312_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962312-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Among these measures were the <a href="/wiki/Homestead_Acts" title="Homestead Acts">Homestead Act</a>, which would have given 160 acres of public land to settlers who remained on the land for five years, and the <a href="/wiki/Morrill_Land-Grant_Acts" title="Morrill Land-Grant Acts">Morrill Act</a>, which would have granted public lands to establish <a href="/wiki/Land-grant_university" title="Land-grant university">land-grant colleges</a>. Buchanan argued that these acts were unconstitutional.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker2004117–118_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker2004117–118-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Foreign policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Buchanan took office with an ambitious foreign policy, designed to establish U.S. <a href="/wiki/Hegemony" title="Hegemony">hegemony</a> over Central America at the expense of Great Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith197569–70_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith197569–70-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> He hoped to re-negotiate the <a href="/wiki/Clayton%E2%80%93Bulwer_Treaty" title="Clayton–Bulwer Treaty">Clayton–Bulwer Treaty</a>, which he thought limited U.S. influence in the region. He also sought to establish American protectorates over the Mexican states of <a href="/wiki/Chihuahua_(state)" title="Chihuahua (state)">Chihuahua</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sonora" title="Sonora">Sonora</a>, and most importantly, he hoped to achieve his long-term goal of acquiring Cuba. After long negotiations with the British, he convinced them to cede the <a href="/wiki/Bay_Islands_Department" title="Bay Islands Department">Bay Islands</a> to <a href="/wiki/Honduras" title="Honduras">Honduras</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Mosquito_Coast" title="Mosquito Coast">Mosquito Coast</a> to <a href="/wiki/Nicaragua" title="Nicaragua">Nicaragua</a>. However, Buchanan's ambitions in Cuba and Mexico were largely blocked by the House of Representatives.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker2004107–112_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker2004107–112-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan also considered buying Alaska from the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a>, as a colony for Mormon settlers, but he and the Russians were unable to agree upon a price. In China, the administration won trade concessions in the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Tientsin" title="Treaty of Tientsin">Treaty of Tientsin</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith197574–75_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith197574–75-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> In 1858, Buchanan ordered the <a href="/wiki/Paraguay_expedition" title="Paraguay expedition">Paraguay expedition</a> to punish <a href="/wiki/Paraguay" title="Paraguay">Paraguay</a> for firing on the <a href="/wiki/USS_Water_Witch_(1851)" title="USS Water Witch (1851)">USS&#160;<i>Water Witch</i></a>, and the expedition resulted in a Paraguayan apology and payment of an indemnity.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker2004107–112_79-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker2004107–112-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> The chiefs of <a href="/wiki/Raiatea" title="Raiatea">Raiatea</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tahaa" class="mw-redirect" title="Tahaa">Tahaa</a> in the South Pacific, refusing to accept the rule of King <a href="/wiki/Tamatoa_V" title="Tamatoa V">Tamatoa V</a>, unsuccessfully petitioned the United States to accept the islands under a <a href="/wiki/Protectorate" title="Protectorate">protectorate</a> in June 1858.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFlude2012393–413_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlude2012393–413-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan was offered a herd of elephants by King <a href="/wiki/Rama_IV" class="mw-redirect" title="Rama IV">Rama IV</a> of <a href="/wiki/Thailand" title="Thailand">Siam</a>, though the letter arrived after Buchanan's departure from office. As Buchanan's successor, Lincoln declined the King's offer, citing the unsuitable climate.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> Other presidential pets included a pair of bald eagles and a Newfoundland dog.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Covode_Committee">Covode Committee</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Covode Committee">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In March 1860, the House impaneled the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_Select_Committee_to_Investigate_Alleged_Corruptions_in_Government" title="United States House Select Committee to Investigate Alleged Corruptions in Government">Covode Committee</a> to investigate the administration for alleged impeachable offenses, such as bribery and extortion of representatives. The committee, three Republicans and two Democrats, was accused by Buchanan's supporters of being nakedly partisan; they charged its chairman, Republican Rep. <a href="/wiki/John_Covode" title="John Covode">John Covode</a>, with acting on a personal grudge from a disputed land grant designed to benefit Covode's railroad company.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962338_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962338-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> The Democratic committee members, as well as Democratic witnesses, were enthusiastic in their condemnation of Buchanan.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962338–9_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962338–9-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrossman200378_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrossman200378-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The committee was unable to establish grounds for impeaching Buchanan; however, the majority report issued on June 17 alleged corruption and abuse of power among members of his cabinet. The report also included accusations from Republicans that Buchanan had attempted to bribe members of Congress, in connection with the pro-slavery <a href="/wiki/Lecompton_Constitution" title="Lecompton Constitution">Lecompton Constitution</a> of Kansas. The Democrats pointed out that evidence was scarce, but did not refute the allegations; one of the Democratic members, Rep. <a href="/wiki/James_Carroll_Robinson" title="James Carroll Robinson">James Robinson</a>, stated that he agreed with the Republicans, though he did not sign it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrossman200378_86-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrossman200378-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan claimed to have "passed triumphantly through this ordeal" with complete vindication. Republican operatives distributed thousands of copies of the Covode Committee report throughout the nation as campaign material in that year's presidential election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker2004114–118_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker2004114–118-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962339_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962339-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_of_1860">Election of 1860</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Election of 1860">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1860_United_States_presidential_election" title="1860 United States presidential election">1860 United States presidential election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_C_Breckinridge-04775-restored.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/John_C_Breckinridge-04775-restored.jpg/170px-John_C_Breckinridge-04775-restored.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="228" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/John_C_Breckinridge-04775-restored.jpg/255px-John_C_Breckinridge-04775-restored.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/John_C_Breckinridge-04775-restored.jpg/340px-John_C_Breckinridge-04775-restored.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2050" data-file-height="2755" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/John_C._Breckinridge" title="John C. Breckinridge">John C. Breckinridge</a>, <a href="/wiki/Vice_President_of_the_United_States" title="Vice President of the United States">Vice President of the United States</a> under Buchanan</figcaption></figure>
<p>As he had promised in his inaugural address, Buchanan did not seek re-election. He went so far as to tell his ultimate successor, "If you are as happy in entering the White House as I shall feel on returning to Wheatland [his home], you are a happy man."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/1860_Democratic_National_Conventions#Charleston_Convention" title="1860 Democratic National Conventions">1860 Democratic National Convention</a> convened in April of that year and, though Douglas led after every ballot, he was unable to win the two-thirds majority required. The convention adjourned after 53 ballots, and re-convened in Baltimore in June. After Douglas finally won the nomination, several Southerners refused to accept the outcome, and nominated Vice President Breckinridge as their own candidate. Douglas and Breckinridge agreed on most issues except the protection of slavery. Buchanan, nursing a grudge against Douglas, failed to reconcile the party, and tepidly supported Breckinridge. With the splintering of the Democratic Party, Republican nominee <a href="/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln" title="Abraham Lincoln">Abraham Lincoln</a> won a four-way election that also included <a href="/wiki/John_Bell_(Tennessee_politician)" title="John Bell (Tennessee politician)">John Bell</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_Union_Party_(United_States)" title="Constitutional Union Party (United States)">Constitutional Union Party</a>. Lincoln's support in the North was enough to give him an Electoral College majority. Buchanan became the last Democrat to win a presidential election until <a href="/wiki/Grover_Cleveland" title="Grover Cleveland">Grover Cleveland</a> in 1884.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker2004118–120_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker2004118–120-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As early as October, the army's <a href="/wiki/Commanding_General_of_the_United_States_Army" title="Commanding General of the United States Army">Commanding General</a>, <a href="/wiki/Winfield_Scott" title="Winfield Scott">Winfield Scott</a>, an opponent of Buchanan, warned him that Lincoln's election would likely cause at least seven states to secede from the union. He recommended that massive amounts of federal troops and artillery be deployed to those states to protect federal property, although he also warned that few reinforcements were available. Since 1857, Congress had failed to heed calls for a stronger militia and allowed the army to fall into deplorable condition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962356–358_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962356–358-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> Buchanan distrusted Scott and ignored his recommendations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200476,_133_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200476,_133-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> After Lincoln's election, Buchanan directed War Secretary Floyd to reinforce southern forts with such provisions, arms, and men as were available; however, Floyd persuaded him to revoke the order.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962356–358_91-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962356–358-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Secession">Secession</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Secession">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>With Lincoln's victory, talk of secession and disunion reached a boiling point, putting the burden on Buchanan to address it in his final speech to Congress on December 10. In his message, which was anticipated by both factions, Buchanan denied the right of states to secede but maintained the federal government was without power to prevent them. He placed the blame for the crisis solely on "intemperate interference of the Northern people with the question of slavery in the Southern States," and suggested that if they did not "repeal their unconstitutional and obnoxious enactments ... the injured States, after having first used all peaceful and constitutional means to obtain redress, would be justified in revolutionary resistance to the Government of the Union."<sup id="cite_ref-1860message_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1860message-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Buchanan's only suggestion to solve the crisis was "an explanatory amendment" affirming the constitutionality of slavery in the states, the fugitive slave laws, and <a href="/wiki/Popular_sovereignty" title="Popular sovereignty">popular sovereignty</a> in the territories.<sup id="cite_ref-1860message_93-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1860message-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> His address was sharply criticized both by the North, for its refusal to stop secession, and the South, for denying its right to secede.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962363_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962363-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Five days after the address was delivered, Treasury Secretary <a href="/wiki/Howell_Cobb" title="Howell Cobb">Howell Cobb</a> resigned, as his views had become irreconcilable with the President's.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:US_Secession_map_1861.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Map of U.S. showing two kinds of Union states, two phases of secession and territories" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/US_Secession_map_1861.svg/250px-US_Secession_map_1861.svg.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="153" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/US_Secession_map_1861.svg/375px-US_Secession_map_1861.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/US_Secession_map_1861.svg/500px-US_Secession_map_1861.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="787" data-file-height="483" /></a><figcaption><div class="center" style="width:auto; margin-left:auto; margin-right:auto;"><b>Status of the states, 1861</b></div> <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r981673959">.mw-parser-output .legend{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .legend-color{display:inline-block;min-width:1.25em;height:1.25em;line-height:1.25;margin:1px 0;text-align:center;border:1px solid black;background-color:transparent;color:black}.mw-parser-output .legend-text{}</style><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#A40000; color:white;">&#160;</span>&#160; States that seceded before April 15, 1861</div> <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#EF2929; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160; States that seceded after April 15, 1861</div> <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#FCE94F; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160; Union states that permitted slavery</div> <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#204A87; color:white;">&#160;</span>&#160; Union states that banned slavery</div> <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#D3D7CF; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160; Territories</div></figcaption></figure>
<p>South Carolina, long the most radical Southern state, seceded from the Union on December 20, 1860. However, Unionist sentiment remained strong among many in the South, and Buchanan sought to appeal to the Southern moderates who might prevent secession in other states. He proposed passage of constitutional amendments protecting slavery in the states and territories. He also met with South Carolinian commissioners in an attempt to resolve the situation at <a href="/wiki/Fort_Sumter" title="Fort Sumter">Fort Sumter</a>, which federal forces remained in control of despite its location in Charleston, South Carolina. He refused to dismiss Interior Secretary <a href="/wiki/Jacob_Thompson" title="Jacob Thompson">Jacob Thompson</a> after the latter was chosen as Mississippi's agent to discuss secession, and he refused to fire Secretary of War John B. Floyd despite an embezzlement scandal. Floyd ended up resigning, but not before sending numerous firearms to Southern states, where they eventually fell into the hands of the Confederacy. Despite Floyd's resignation, Buchanan continued to seek the advice of counselors from the Deep South, including <a href="/wiki/Jefferson_Davis" title="Jefferson Davis">Jefferson Davis</a> and <a href="/wiki/William_Henry_Trescot" title="William Henry Trescot">William Henry Trescot</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker2004123–134_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker2004123–134-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Efforts were made in vain by Sen. <a href="/wiki/John_J._Crittenden" title="John J. Crittenden">John J. Crittenden</a>, Rep. <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Corwin" title="Thomas Corwin">Thomas Corwin</a>, and former president <a href="/wiki/John_Tyler" title="John Tyler">John Tyler</a> to negotiate a compromise to stop secession, with Buchanan's support. Failed attempts were also made by a group of governors meeting in New York. Buchanan secretly asked President-elect Lincoln to call for a national referendum on the issue of slavery, but Lincoln declined.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962381–387_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962381–387-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite the efforts of Buchanan and others, six more <a href="/wiki/Slave_states" class="mw-redirect" title="Slave states">slave states</a> seceded by the end of January 1861. Buchanan replaced the departed Southern cabinet members with <a href="/wiki/John_Adams_Dix" title="John Adams Dix">John Adams Dix</a>, <a href="/wiki/Edwin_M._Stanton" class="mw-redirect" title="Edwin M. Stanton">Edwin M. Stanton</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Holt" title="Joseph Holt">Joseph Holt</a>, all of whom were committed to preserving the Union. When Buchanan considered surrendering <a href="/wiki/Fort_Sumter" title="Fort Sumter">Fort Sumter</a>, the new cabinet members threatened to resign, and Buchanan relented. On January 5, Buchanan decided to reinforce Fort Sumter, sending the <i><a href="/wiki/Star_of_the_West" title="Star of the West">Star of the West</a></i> with 250 men and supplies. However, he failed to ask Major <a href="/wiki/Robert_Anderson_(Civil_War)" title="Robert Anderson (Civil War)">Robert Anderson</a> to provide covering fire for the ship, and it was forced to return North without delivering troops or supplies. Buchanan chose not to respond to this act of war, and instead sought to find a compromise to avoid secession. He received a March 3 message from Anderson, that supplies were running low, but the response became Lincoln's to make, as the latter succeeded to the presidency the next day.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker2004135–140_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker2004135–140-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Proposed_constitutional_amendment">Proposed constitutional amendment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Proposed constitutional amendment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>On March 2, 1861, Congress approved an amendment to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Constitution">United States Constitution</a> that would shield "domestic institutions" of the states, including slavery, from the constitutional amendment process and from abolition or interference by Congress. The proposed amendment was submitted to the <a href="/wiki/State_legislature_(United_States)" title="State legislature (United States)">state legislatures</a> for <a href="/wiki/Ratification" title="Ratification">ratification</a>. Commonly known as the <a href="/wiki/Corwin_Amendment" title="Corwin Amendment">Corwin Amendment</a>, it was never ratified by the requisite number of states.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="States_admitted_to_the_Union">States admitted to the Union</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: States admitted to the Union">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Three new states were <a href="/wiki/Admission_to_the_Union" title="Admission to the Union">admitted to the Union</a> while Buchanan was in office:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Minnesota" title="Minnesota">Minnesota</a>&#160;– May 11, 1858<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Oregon" title="Oregon">Oregon</a>&#160;– February 14, 1859<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Kansas" title="Kansas">Kansas</a>&#160;– January 29, 1861</li></ul>
<h2><span id="Post-presidency_.281861.E2.80.931868.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-presidency_(1861–1868)">Post-presidency (1861–1868)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Post-presidency (1861–1868)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:James_Buchanan_-_post_presidency.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/James_Buchanan_-_post_presidency.jpg/200px-James_Buchanan_-_post_presidency.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="258" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/James_Buchanan_-_post_presidency.jpg/300px-James_Buchanan_-_post_presidency.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/James_Buchanan_-_post_presidency.jpg/400px-James_Buchanan_-_post_presidency.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2046" data-file-height="2640" /></a><figcaption>Buchanan in his later years. <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> mid-1860s</figcaption></figure>
<p>The Civil War erupted within two months of Buchanan's retirement. He supported the Union and the war effort, writing to former colleagues that, "the assault upon Sumter was the commencement of war by the Confederate states, and no alternative was left but to prosecute it with vigor on our part."<sup id="cite_ref-Birkner_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Birkner-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> He also wrote a letter to his fellow Pennsylvania Democrats, urging them to "join the many thousands of brave &amp; patriotic volunteers who are already in the field."<sup id="cite_ref-Birkner_102-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Birkner-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan was dedicated to defending his actions prior to the Civil War, which was referred to by some as "Buchanan's War".<sup id="cite_ref-Birkner_102-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Birkner-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> He received threatening letters daily, and stores displayed Buchanan's likeness with the eyes inked red, a noose drawn around his neck and the word "TRAITOR" written across his forehead. The Senate proposed a resolution of condemnation which ultimately failed, and newspapers accused him of colluding with the Confederacy. His former cabinet members, five of whom had been given jobs in the Lincoln administration, refused to defend Buchanan publicly.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962408–413_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962408–413-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan became distraught by the vitriolic attacks levied against him, and fell sick and depressed. In October 1862, he defended himself in an exchange of letters with <a href="/wiki/Winfield_Scott" title="Winfield Scott">Winfield Scott</a>, published in the <i><a href="/wiki/National_Intelligencer" title="National Intelligencer">National Intelligencer</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962417–418_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962417–418-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> He soon began writing his fullest public defense, in the form of his memoir <i>Mr. Buchanan's Administration on the Eve of Rebellion</i>, which was published in 1866.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker2004142–143_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker2004142–143-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><span class="anchor" id="Death"></span>
Soon after the publication of the memoir, Buchanan caught a <a href="/wiki/Common_cold" title="Common cold">cold</a> in May 1868, which quickly worsened due to his advanced age. He died on June 1, 1868, of <a href="/wiki/Respiratory_failure" title="Respiratory failure">respiratory failure</a> at the age of 77 at his home at <a href="/wiki/Wheatland_(Lancaster)" class="mw-redirect" title="Wheatland (Lancaster)">Wheatland</a>. He was interred in <a href="/wiki/Woodward_Hill_Cemetery" title="Woodward Hill Cemetery">Woodward Hill Cemetery</a> in Lancaster.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker2004142–143_105-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker2004142–143-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_views">Political views</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Political views">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:George_Peter_Alexander_Healy_-_James_Buchanan_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/George_Peter_Alexander_Healy_-_James_Buchanan_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/220px-George_Peter_Alexander_Healy_-_James_Buchanan_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="290" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/George_Peter_Alexander_Healy_-_James_Buchanan_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/330px-George_Peter_Alexander_Healy_-_James_Buchanan_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/George_Peter_Alexander_Healy_-_James_Buchanan_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/440px-George_Peter_Alexander_Healy_-_James_Buchanan_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4828" data-file-height="6354" /></a><figcaption>James Buchanan (1859) by <a href="/wiki/George_Healy" class="mw-redirect" title="George Healy">George Healy</a> as seen in the <a href="/wiki/National_Portrait_Gallery_(United_States)" title="National Portrait Gallery (United States)">National Portrait Gallery</a> in Washington, DC</figcaption></figure>
<p>Buchanan was often considered by anti-slavery northerners a "<a href="/wiki/Doughface" title="Doughface">doughface</a>", a northerner with pro-southern principles.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStampp199048_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStampp199048-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> Shortly after his election, he said that the "great object" of his administration was "to arrest, if possible, the agitation of the Slavery question in the North and to destroy sectional parties".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStampp199048_106-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStampp199048-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> Although Buchanan was personally opposed to slavery,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200430_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200430-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> he believed that the abolitionists were preventing the solution to the slavery problem. He stated, "Before [the abolitionists] commenced this agitation, a very large and growing party existed in several of the slave states in favor of the gradual abolition of slavery; and now not a voice is heard there in support of such a measure. The abolitionists have postponed the emancipation of the slaves in three or four states for at least half a century."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962150_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962150-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> In deference to the intentions of the typical slaveholder, he was willing to provide the benefit of the doubt. In his third annual message to Congress, the president claimed that the slaves were "treated with kindness and humanity. ... Both the philanthropy and the self-interest of the master have combined to produce this humane result."<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan thought restraint was the essence of good self-government. He believed the constitution comprised "... restraints, imposed not by arbitrary authority, but by the people upon themselves and their representatives. ... In an enlarged view, the people's interests may seem identical, but to the eye of local and sectional prejudice, they always appear to be conflicting ... and the jealousies that will perpetually arise can be repressed only by the mutual forbearance which pervades the constitution."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962143_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962143-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> Regarding slavery and the Constitution, he stated: "Although in Pennsylvania we are all opposed to slavery in the abstract, we can never violate the constitutional compact we have with our sister states. Their rights will be held sacred by us. Under the constitution it is their own question; and there let it remain."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962150_107-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962150-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Wheatland,_stately_home_of_President_James_Buchanan.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="James Buchanan House, 1120 Marietta Avenue Lancaster Township" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/Wheatland%2C_stately_home_of_President_James_Buchanan.jpg/220px-Wheatland%2C_stately_home_of_President_James_Buchanan.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="81" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/Wheatland%2C_stately_home_of_President_James_Buchanan.jpg/330px-Wheatland%2C_stately_home_of_President_James_Buchanan.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/Wheatland%2C_stately_home_of_President_James_Buchanan.jpg/440px-Wheatland%2C_stately_home_of_President_James_Buchanan.jpg 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="333" /></a><figcaption>James Buchanan's House, Wheatland.</figcaption></figure>
<p>One of the prominent issues of the day was <a href="/wiki/Tariffs_in_United_States_history" class="mw-redirect" title="Tariffs in United States history">tariffs</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJurinski16–17_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJurinski16–17-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Buchanan was conflicted by <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">free trade</a> as well as <a href="/wiki/Protective_tariff" title="Protective tariff">prohibitive tariffs</a>, since either would benefit one section of the country to the detriment of the other. As a senator from Pennsylvania, he said: "I am viewed as the strongest advocate of protection in other states, whilst I am denounced as its enemy in Pennsylvania."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962144_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962144-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan was also torn between his desire to expand the country for the general welfare of the nation, and to guarantee the rights of the people settling particular areas. On territorial expansion, he said, "What, sir? Prevent the people from crossing the <a href="/wiki/Rocky_Mountains" title="Rocky Mountains">Rocky Mountains</a>? You might just as well command the <a href="/wiki/Niagara_Falls" title="Niagara Falls">Niagara</a> not to flow. We must fulfill our destiny."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962147_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962147-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> On the resulting spread of slavery, through unconditional expansion, he stated: "I feel a strong repugnance by any act of mine to extend the present limits of the Union over a new slave-holding territory." For instance, he hoped the acquisition of Texas would "be the means of limiting, not enlarging, the dominion of slavery."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962147_112-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962147-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In 1818, Buchanan met Anne Caroline Coleman at a grand ball in Lancaster, and the two began courting. Anne was the daughter of wealthy iron manufacturer Robert Coleman. She was also the sister-in-law of Philadelphia judge <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Hemphill" title="Joseph Hemphill">Joseph Hemphill</a>, one of Buchanan's colleagues. By 1819, the two were engaged, but spent little time together. Buchanan was busy with his law firm and political projects during the <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1819" title="Panic of 1819">Panic of 1819</a>, which took him away from Coleman for weeks at a time. Rumors abounded, as some suggested that he was marrying her only for money; others said he was involved with other (unidentified) women. Letters from Coleman revealed she was aware of several rumors.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> She broke off the engagement, and soon afterward, on December 9, 1819, suddenly died.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1955_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1955-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> Buchanan wrote to her father for permission to attend the funeral, which was refused.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:William_Rufus_King_edited..jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/William_Rufus_King_edited..jpg/150px-William_Rufus_King_edited..jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="196" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/William_Rufus_King_edited..jpg/225px-William_Rufus_King_edited..jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/William_Rufus_King_edited..jpg/300px-William_Rufus_King_edited..jpg 2x" data-file-width="352" data-file-height="461" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/William_R._King" title="William R. King">William Rufus DeVane King</a>, Buchanan's roommate and speculated partner</figcaption></figure>
<p>After Coleman's death, Buchanan never courted another woman. At the time of her funeral, he said that, "I feel happiness has fled from me forever."<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> During his presidency, an orphaned niece, <a href="/wiki/Harriet_Lane" title="Harriet Lane">Harriet Lane</a>, whom he had adopted, served as official White House hostess.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> There was an unfounded rumor that he had an affair with President Polk's widow, <a href="/wiki/Sarah_Childress_Polk" title="Sarah Childress Polk">Sarah Childress Polk</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan's lifelong bachelorhood after Anne Coleman's death has drawn interest and speculation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200425–26_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200425–26-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> Some conjecture that Anne's death merely served to deflect questions about Buchanan's sexuality and bachelorhood.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_116-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> Several writers have surmised that he was <a href="/wiki/Homosexual" class="mw-redirect" title="Homosexual">homosexual</a>, including James W. Loewen,<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> Robert P. Watson, and Shelley Ross.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERoss198886–91_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoss198886–91-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWatson2012233_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatson2012233-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> One of his biographers, Jean Baker, suggests that Buchanan was <a href="/wiki/Celibate" class="mw-redirect" title="Celibate">celibate</a>, if not <a href="/wiki/Asexuality" title="Asexuality">asexual</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200426_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200426-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan had a close relationship with <a href="/wiki/William_R._King" title="William R. King">William Rufus King</a>, which became a popular target of gossip. King was an Alabama politician who briefly served as vice president under <a href="/wiki/Franklin_Pierce" title="Franklin Pierce">Franklin Pierce</a>. Buchanan and King lived together in a Washington boardinghouse and attended social functions together from 1834 until 1844. Such a living arrangement was then common, though King once referred to the relationship as a "communion".<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_118-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> Andrew Jackson called King "Miss Nancy" and Buchanan's Postmaster General <a href="/wiki/Aaron_V._Brown" title="Aaron V. Brown">Aaron V. Brown</a> referred to King as Buchanan's "better half", "wife", and "Aunt Fancy".<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200475_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200475-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> Loewen indicated that Buchanan late in life wrote a letter acknowledging that he might marry a woman who could accept his "lack of ardent or romantic affection".<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> Catherine Thompson, the wife of cabinet member <a href="/wiki/Jacob_Thompson" title="Jacob Thompson">Jacob Thompson</a>, later noted that "there was something unhealthy in the president's attitude."<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_118-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> King died of tuberculosis shortly after Pierce's inauguration, four years before Buchanan became president. Buchanan described him as "among the best, the purest and most consistent public men I have known".<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_118-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> Biographer Baker opines that both men's nieces may have destroyed correspondence between the two men. However, she believes that their surviving letters illustrate only "the affection of a special friendship".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker200425–26_119-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200425–26-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Historical_reputation">Historical reputation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=32" title="Edit section: Historical reputation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Though Buchanan predicted that "history will vindicate my memory,"<sup id="cite_ref-state_park_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-state_park-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> historians have criticized Buchanan for his unwillingness or inability to act in the face of secession. <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">Historical rankings of presidents of the United States</a> without exception place Buchanan among the least successful presidents.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> When scholars are surveyed, he ranks at or near the bottom in terms of vision/agenda-setting,<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> domestic leadership, foreign policy leadership,<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> moral authority,<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> and positive historical significance of their legacy.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint noexcerpt Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:NOTRS" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:NOTRS"><span title="Inaugural source from UK &quot;experts&quot; much less credible than established source. (July 2022)">better&#160;source&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> According to surveys taken by American scholars and political scientists between 1948 and 1982, Buchanan ranks every time among the worst presidents of the United States, alongside <a href="/wiki/Ulysses_S._Grant" title="Ulysses S. Grant">Grant</a>, <a href="/wiki/Warren_G._Harding" title="Warren G. Harding">Harding</a>, <a href="/wiki/Millard_Fillmore" title="Millard Fillmore">Fillmore</a> and <a href="/wiki/Richard_Nixon" title="Richard Nixon">Nixon</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Buchanan biographer Philip S. Klein focused in 1962, during the <a href="/wiki/Civil_rights_movement" title="Civil rights movement">Civil Rights movement</a>, upon challenges Buchanan faced:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Buchanan assumed leadership ... when an unprecedented wave of angry passion was sweeping over the nation. That he held the hostile sections in check during these revolutionary times was in itself a remarkable achievement. His weaknesses in the stormy years of his presidency were magnified by enraged partisans of the North and South. His many talents, which in a quieter era might have gained for him a place among the great presidents, were quickly overshadowed by the cataclysmic events of civil war and by the towering Abraham Lincoln.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlein1962429_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein1962429-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Biographer Jean Baker is less charitable to Buchanan, saying in 2004:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Americans have conveniently misled themselves about the presidency of James Buchanan, preferring to classify him as indecisive and inactive&#160;... In fact Buchanan's failing during the crisis over the Union was not inactivity, but rather his partiality for the South, a favoritism that bordered on disloyalty in an officer pledged to defend all the United States. He was that most dangerous of chief executives, a stubborn, mistaken ideologue whose principles held no room for compromise. His experience in government had only rendered him too self-confident to consider other views. In his betrayal of the national trust, Buchanan came closer to committing treason than any other president in American history.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaker2004141_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker2004141-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote><p>Other historians, such as Robert May, argued that his politics were "anything but pro-slavery",<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> nevertheless, a very negative view is to be found in <a href="/wiki/Michael_Birkner" title="Michael Birkner">Michael Birkner</a>'s works about Buchanan.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> For <a href="/wiki/Lori_Cox_Han" title="Lori Cox Han">Lori Cox Han</a>, he ranks among scholars "as either the worst president in [American] history or as part of a lowest ranking failure category".<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup>
</p><h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Memorials">Memorials</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=33" title="Edit section: Memorials">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>A bronze and granite <a href="/wiki/James_Buchanan_Memorial" title="James Buchanan Memorial">memorial</a> near the southeast corner of Washington, D.C.'s <a href="/wiki/Meridian_Hill_Park" title="Meridian Hill Park">Meridian Hill Park</a> was designed by architect William Gorden Beecher and sculpted by Maryland artist <a href="/wiki/Hans_Schuler" title="Hans Schuler">Hans Schuler</a>. It was commissioned in 1916 but not approved by the U.S. Congress until 1918, and not completed and unveiled until June 26, 1930. The memorial features a statue of Buchanan, bookended by male and female classical figures representing law and diplomacy, with engraved text reading: "The incorruptible statesman whose walk was upon the mountain ranges of the law," a quote from a member of Buchanan's cabinet, <a href="/wiki/Jeremiah_S._Black" title="Jeremiah S. Black">Jeremiah S. Black</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrauss2016213_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrauss2016213-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:BuchananmonmtDC.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/BuchananmonmtDC.JPG/250px-BuchananmonmtDC.JPG" decoding="async" width="250" height="79" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/BuchananmonmtDC.JPG/375px-BuchananmonmtDC.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/BuchananmonmtDC.JPG/500px-BuchananmonmtDC.JPG 2x" data-file-width="3138" data-file-height="992" /></a><figcaption>Buchanan memorial, Washington, D.C.</figcaption></figure>
<p>An earlier monument was constructed in 1907–1908 and dedicated in 1911, on the site of Buchanan's birthplace in <a href="/wiki/Buchanan%27s_Birthplace_State_Park" title="Buchanan&#39;s Birthplace State Park">Stony Batter, Pennsylvania</a>. Part of the original 18.5-acre (75,000&#160;m<sup>2</sup>) memorial site is a 250-ton pyramid structure that stands on the site of the original cabin where Buchanan was born. The monument was designed to show the original weathered surface of the native rubble and mortar.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Three counties are named in his honor, in <a href="/wiki/Buchanan_County,_Iowa" title="Buchanan County, Iowa">Iowa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Buchanan_County,_Missouri" title="Buchanan County, Missouri">Missouri</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Buchanan_County,_Virginia" title="Buchanan County, Virginia">Virginia</a>. Another in Texas was christened in 1858 but renamed <a href="/wiki/Stephens_County,_Texas" title="Stephens County, Texas">Stephens County</a>, after the newly elected Vice President of the Confederate States of America, <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Stephens" class="mw-redirect" title="Alexander Stephens">Alexander Stephens</a>, in 1861.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBeatty2001310_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeatty2001310-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> The city of <a href="/wiki/Buchanan,_Michigan" title="Buchanan, Michigan">Buchanan, Michigan</a>, was also named after him.<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> Several other communities are named after him: the unincorporated community of <a href="/wiki/Buchanan,_Indiana" title="Buchanan, Indiana">Buchanan, Indiana</a>, the city of <a href="/wiki/Buchanan,_Georgia" title="Buchanan, Georgia">Buchanan, Georgia</a>, the town of <a href="/wiki/Buchanan,_Wisconsin" title="Buchanan, Wisconsin">Buchanan, Wisconsin</a>, and the townships of <a href="/wiki/Buchanan_Township,_Michigan" title="Buchanan Township, Michigan">Buchanan Township, Michigan</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Buchanan,_Missouri" title="Buchanan, Missouri">Buchanan, Missouri</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/James_Buchanan_High_School" title="James Buchanan High School">James Buchanan High School</a> is a small, rural high school located on the outskirts of his childhood hometown, <a href="/wiki/Mercersburg,_Pennsylvania" title="Mercersburg, Pennsylvania">Mercersburg, Pennsylvania</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Popular_culture_depictions">Popular culture depictions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=34" title="Edit section: Popular culture depictions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Buchanan and his legacy are central to the film <i><a href="/wiki/Raising_Buchanan" title="Raising Buchanan">Raising Buchanan</a></i> (2019). He is portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Auberjonois" title="René Auberjonois">René Auberjonois</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="See_also">See also</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=James_Buchanan&amp;action=edit&amp;section=35" title="Edit section: See also">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">Historical rankings of presidents of the United States</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="List of presidents of the United States">List of presidents of the United States</a></li>
